Ben & Jerry’s CEO: democracy is on the ballot, and business must show up
2026-10-31

Ben & Jerry’s CEO: democracy is on the ballot, and business must show up

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Ben & Jerry’s is a proud Vermont-based business. We’re a global company, but the U.S. is our home and where we have a long history of campaigning for democracy. We believe that when businesses step up, particularly at moments like this, we can help shift the scales. It’s clear to me, as one of the stewards of this business, that we have a responsibility to help uphold democracy and empower communities to participate in it. It’s the right thing to do, and no business thrives where democracy doesn’t. Any slide toward authoritarianism threatens the economic environment every business depends on. Ben & Jerry’s is built on the model of Linked Prosperity, the belief that the success of a business should not be measured solely by the economic value it creates, but also by the value it creates for the communities it serves. This philosophy is embedded in how we operate because businesses do not exist in a vacuum. They rely on strong, stable communities, and those communities depend on institutions like free and fair elections, which ensure people’s voices are heard and their votes respected. Now, those institutions are under threat, freedom of expression is under attack, and the right to vote is under pressure. Many people feel disconnected from the politics that are supposed to represent them, shut out of decision-making and uncertain if their voices even matter. This work goes back a long way for Ben & Jerry’s. In 2016, our Empower Mint flavor supported voting rights alongside Rev. William Barber II and the North Carolina NAACP; in 2018 we backed Florida’s Amendment 4 campaign to restore voting rights to people with prior felony convictions. More recently, Change Is Brewing and Churn Out the Vote have supported Black voter participation and practical voter information. We have learned a lot along the way from people and organizations who understand these issues better than we do. That has shaped our approach; we’re smart enough to know we don’t know everything, so we work with incredible partners who are working day in and day out on the issues at a grassroots level. The ambition then is to put the reach and resources of Ben & Jerry’s behind their work and bring our fans to it too. I won’t pretend that balancing a social mission with a product and economic mission is easy. Since the beginning there has been debate about what our social mission asks of us and how we put it into practice. Sometimes that tension is uncomfortable, but I have come to believe that there is no progress without tension. The genius of our governance model is that it gives the social mission an equal role. We can balance each part of our three-part mission because it is baked into how we make decisions. That model can sometimes surface and create that tension, but I would rather have that debate than let the mission become something we talk about without really testing ourselves against it. This year, we have worked with our long-term partners at the American Civil Liberties Union to educate voters, help people get ready to vote, and protect their right to do so. Millions have viewed our content and over 175,000 people have taken action with Ben & Jerry’s this election season. Our midterms campaign, supported by our flavor Razz Up!, uses our voice and our ice cream to bring attention to the work of our partners. Following the lead of Black Voters Matter, Community Change and the National Korean American Service & Education Consortium, we have shown up in communities where restrictive voting laws are reinforcing inequality, reaching nearly 10,000 people through Step Razz Up! events in Detroit and Houston and supporting voter engagement events across North Carolina. We have put financial resources behind the work too. Our Scoop Shop initiative, Waffle Lotta Good, raised nearly $100,000 in October for grassroots groups working to protect democracy. Our commitment also stretches beyond this election: our $3 million unrestricted investment in People’s Action Institute will support community organizing well beyond Election Day. In August, on the anniversary of the 1965 Voting Rights Act, we took a pointed pun, ‘Fudge Fascism,’ to Washington, D.C. When the stakes are so high, hedged language isn’t going to cut it, and we have always believed that serious issues don’t have to be communicated in ways that feel distant or inaccessible. Razz Up! and our broader pro-democracy work is part of a social mission that has been built into Ben & Jerry’s over decades. We’re able to do this work because of colleagues and partners who challenge us, bring expertise we don’t have, and keep pushing us to live up to the values of this company. Whatever happens on Tuesday, I hope more businesses wake up to the role they can play. We shouldn’t wait until the next election is around the corner. For businesses wondering where to begin, Civic Alliance is a good place to start. It brings businesses together around a nonpartisan commitment to democracy and gives companies a way to learn from others already doing this work. The work begins now. Local elections happen every year and play an important role. Plus, 2028 will come around quickly. Our responsibility to the ideals of democracy does not end at the ballot box. Business has a long-term role to play in its success, because democracy is a habit, not a one-time act. Our AI agent experience: we scrapped the multiple $60,000 salary marketing jobs and hired one person at a far higher salary
2026-10-31

Our AI agent experience: we scrapped the multiple $60,000 salary marketing jobs and hired one person at a far higher salary

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
In July, Accenture published guidance for companies alarmed by their AI bills. Avoid blanket usage cuts, it said. Don’t tell employees to use less AI. Route work to the cheapest model that does the job. It also noted that in nearly every deployment it had examined, including its own, fewer than 10% of users and workflows drive the vast majority of the bill. On Oct. 1, according to an internal announcement obtained by 404 Media, Accenture began centrally funding $29 a month per person toward Claude; anything above that had to be charged to a project. Internal messages seen by the publication show employees who had been spending $500 to $1,000 a month, and one who said their access was revoked entirely. Accenture says reporting that it has put a firm limit on its people’s access to AI tools is incorrect. Take the company at its word, but the distinction is real, and the exposure is real: fixed-price contracts are more than 65% of Accenture’s bookings, so token costs land on its own margin. But from where an employee sits, the distinction is thin, and Accenture’s own paper records the pressure. In some of its environments, it wrote, requests to lift individual spending limits arrive by the hundreds each day. Companies everywhere are asking the same question: how many tokens should we give each employee? It is the wrong question, and it is leading managers to decisions they will regret. The right question is what a finished piece of work costs, before AI and after. The answer is usually so lopsided that the argument ends there. Here is an answer. One of our portfolio companies needed a new website. The agency quote was $60,000. Instead, three people from the business team — none of whom had ever built a website — did it in three days, and the result was better than what the agency proposed. It worked properly on Windows and Mac computers, on iPhones and Android handsets, and on Chinese phones running HarmonyOS — none of which the team knew to ask for. The agents also produced high-resolution versions of the company’s logos, made its marketing material consistent for the first time, and found customer testimonials nobody knew existed, along with awards the company did not know it had won. The tokens cost a few hundred dollars. Here is a second. Preparing a board presentation on a company we wanted to acquire, we asked AI only to reformat the slides. It did that. It also fact-checked them, which we had not requested, and found several material errors. Then it reached the slide carrying our offer price and told us the price was too high, suggesting we cut it by 40%. We had not asked for its opinion. We checked the reasoning and lowered the bid. Consider what this does to hiring. We were about to take on several entry-level marketing people at around $60,000 each. We hired one instead and paid considerably more. That one non-technical person does work the whole group could not have done: designing the company’s collateral and building analytics software to measure our customers’ operations against industry peers. Notice what these have in common. It is not that people worked faster. Three people who could not build a website built one. A marketing hire who does not write software is shipping a software product. The change is not productivity but capability: work that used to require a specialist can increasingly be done by whoever understands the business problem. We should be honest about what that means. Those are jobs that will not exist. One of us has a close friend, a graphic designer, laid off with two colleagues when new owners decided AI could do what the graphics department did. But a company that refuses these tools does not save those jobs. It loses them later anyway, along with its own. So why is everyone capping? Because the bills arrived, they were larger than expected, and almost nobody can guess what an afternoon of their own AI use costs. AI is sold by the token. A token is roughly three-quarters of a word, and everything you send the system and everything it sends back is counted and billed. A million tokens — about 750,000 words, or eight or nine novels — costs between $2 and $8. A typical office worker asking 30 questions a day uses about 1.3 million tokens a month, roughly $6 of usage. That is why software companies sell $25 seats and still make money. Your average employee is not the problem and never was. Heavy users are different. When people set AI agents to work — reading files, checking numbers, retrying when something fails — one task can consume 50,000 to 500,000 tokens. Call it $12,000 a year for your most intensive person, which is what the heaviest users in that reporting were spending. Is that a good deal? Jellyfish, which measures what software teams actually finish, studied 12,000 developers at 200 companies this year. Among the lightest users, one finished job cost 28 cents in tokens; among the heaviest, $89; in other words, the heaviest users spent almost 320 times more than the lightest, which is like comparing the concert ticket sales of Taylor Swift’s Eras Tour to those of JoJo Siwa. It does not settle the decision. The median developer in that study ran about $52 a month in tokens. The 90th percentile ran $691 — a gap of roughly $7,700 a year, against a salary many times that, for twice the output. The study is observational, so it shows association rather than cause. But the direction is not close. The marginal token is worth less, and still worth far more than it costs. And the bill understates the cost. One of us modeled AI deployments in four industries — bank fraud detection, pharmaceutical research, chemical discovery and chemical manufacturing — comparing what companies are billed against what those systems cost once repeated attempts, idle capacity, monitoring and staff time checking output are counted. In all four, the token bill was about a quarter of the total. Cut the bill and you cut the quarter you can see, leaving the expensive three-quarters alone. So measure outcomes, not consumption. The same firm’s published example is a good one: a quarter-end deck that took 15 hours across three analysts now takes two hours for one person, at a token cost of a couple hundred dollars. The token line on that work is trivial. The labor line it replaced is not. Only one of them is visible when you budget per head. New York offers a warning about writing the rules prematurely. When the state imposed extremely heavy licensing requirements on cryptocurrency firms in 2015, before anyone understood the industry, a wave of exchanges left and never came back. The rules were not badly intended, but they were premature, written by people who would not bear the cost of being wrong. Business, too, should not regulate prematurely. Companies are now doing this to themselves: approval workflows, restricted tool lists, per-person caps. A cap punishes your heaviest user, who is probably your most productive one. And every one of these is a rule written in 2026 that will still bind in 2028, when the facts have moved. Universities once banned AI. Now they require it, and they pay for the licenses. Harvard Business School hands its MBA students ChatGPT and Claude. Darden encourages their use on assignments. Pace University — not an elite outlier, a practical school that sends graduates into New York firms — gives every student a Gemini subscription. A survey taken a year ago would have found most schools policing these tools. That is already the old world. They changed because employers did: when we hire recent graduates, proficiency with foundation models is the single most important skill we screen for. This is a second industrial revolution, and the gains will be of the same order. Overspending is recoverable next quarter. Being left behind is not. From Karl Marx’s vampire to Warren Buffett’s moat, the crumbling gothic castle has been capitalism’s favorite metaphor for 250 years
2026-10-31

From Karl Marx’s vampire to Warren Buffett’s moat, the crumbling gothic castle has been capitalism’s favorite metaphor for 250 years

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Markets are ushering in a particularly gothic Halloween as the AI trade exposes a crumbling aristocratic pile of early 21st century capitalism. Wall Street has been describing itself in castle-adjacent terms for years. Jamie Dimon has long talked to JPMorgan shareholders about the bank’s “fortress balance sheet.” His 2015 letter set aside a section to answer the question of what the phrase even meant. By 2021, he’d added a second castle word, warning that “the moats that protect this company are not particularly deep.” The moat came from Warren Buffett. In a 1999 Fortune article with Carol Loomis, he argued that the key to investing is a company’s competitive advantage and how durable it is, and that the businesses worth owning have wide moats around them. The gothic novel’s favorite picture of danger is a castle falling apart, and for about 250 years the two have traded images: the castle and the moat, the vampire, the creature built in a lab. This Halloween season has them rushing to the front of the conversation, with a two-day rout in tech stocks on Thursday and Friday turning on whether the AI giants’ moats will hold, as gothic horror booms on bookshelves and movie screens. Horror took 15.5% of the domestic box office in 2025, according to The Numbers, and U.S. horror book sales rose 7% through the first nine months of 2025, per Circana BookScan, while adult fiction overall grew just 1%. The New York Times asked Friday why every book is gothic now, with publishers putting the label on everything from family sagas to space operas, and several film critics commenting that Nathan Fielder’s smash-hit business-scandal documentaryYou Can See Everything is a twisted, even gothic romance in the tradition of Alfred Hitchcock. The selling started with Alphabet. The stock fell 12.6% Thursday, wiping out roughly half a trillion dollars in market value, after the Google parent’s quarterly report. Revenue from the core search unit fell short, partly because of the popularity of less-profitable “AI mode” searches and the company burning cash faster than expected. Its free cash flow came in at negative $9.1 billion, against the negative $2.23 billion analysts expected, per FactSet. The S&P 500’s worst performer was software giant ServiceNow, which dropped 27% the same day on slowing revenue growth and disappointing guidance. By Friday morning, it was Amazon’s and Intel’s turn, with Intel down roughly 25% before the opening bell. Big Tech lost nearly $900 billion in market value after another sharp rise in AI spending in the third quarter, the Financial Times calculated. Reddit, whose shares fell more than 22% premarket Friday, is pulling up the drawbridge: it’s moving to a logged-in experience as AI eats search. The irony is spooking investors. AI is eating into the businesses that throw off the cash, and the cash is going into AI anyway. The hyperscalers have raised $326 billion in stock and bonds this year to pay for the build-out, more than triple 2025, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence data. The question underneath it is the one Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok put to clients the morning Alphabet fell. “With AI disruption potentially around the corner, there is a lot of discussion about moats at the moment,” he wrote in his Daily Spark note Thursday. He built on finance professor Aswath Damodaran, the “dean of valuation,” who compiled a list of sources of competitive advantage, including brand strength, switching costs, network effects, cost advantages and legal protection. Then he boiled it down to an AI moat checklist of three questions: does the company own data its rivals can’t get, is its product part of how customers do their jobs every day, and can it charge more without customers walking away? Judging by that third test, the companies selling AI don’t seem to measure up. OpenAI’s new GPT-6.1 Terra model costs up to half as much per token as the version before it. An index from expense-management firm Ramp, which looked at what companies actually pay Anthropic and OpenAI, shows token prices down roughly 35% since the end of June, in what Axios called a price war. The companies paying for AI aren’t getting much back yet, either. In an Oct. 24 note, Slok sent a tremor through Wall Street terminals with an argument that AI “has delivered zero financial payoff to corporate America outside of tech.” Revenue per worker and profit margins are rising for the Magnificent Seven and flat for the rest of the S&P 500, he noted, and mainstream companies are starting to treat token spending as a cost center that weighs on margins rather than a source of growth. Xavier Aldana Reyes, a researcher at the Manchester Centre for Gothic Studies, told the Times that interest in the gothic tends to surge during periods of social anxiety and political turmoil. Literary agent Mary C. Moore put the genre’s subject more bluntly, telling the paper that “Gothics are all about exposing what is rotten at the core of something shiny/wealthy.” The genre was invented by a scion of wealth who knew the old order from inside and drew a salary without working at all. Horace Walpole was the son of UK Prime Minister Robert Walpole, and hisThe Castle of Otranto, published in 1764, is generally regarded as the first gothic novel. He passed it off as a translation of an obscure Italian work, found in the library of an old Catholic family in northern England and printed at Naples in 1529. But it was really his dramatization of the aristocratic system being usurped by the “greedy” new bourgeoisie, per a 2014 paper by Çankaya University’s Ertuğrul Koç and Neslihan Atcan Altan. The literary historian Robert Miles has written that the modern novel itself was understood to have been revolutionized by print technology, which made the circulating library possible, and explains Walpole’s pre-modern faux origin story. We all know which gothic tales came next. In her 1831 introduction to Frankenstein, Mary Shelley said the idea came to her in June 1816 after she overheard Lord Byron and Percy Shelley discussing galvanic experiments. A 2018 piece republished by Live Science made the comparison directly: electrical life stirred up the same range of arguments then that AI does now. Karl Marx made the trade official. In Capital, in 1867, he wrote that capital is “dead labour, that, vampire-like, only lives by sucking living labour,” an image that turns up across 30 years of his writing. Bram Stoker’s Dracula created the great modern vampire myth in 1897, landing as a depression in agriculture threatened the fortunes and estates of Britain’s noblemen, even as a cheap agricultural products flooded in from booming American farms. The literary critic Franco Moretti read the count as a businessman. In a 1982 essay in New Left Review, he described Stoker’s Dracula as a rational entrepreneur who invests his gold to expand his dominion and conquer the City of London. In more recent times, Matt Taibbi’s 2009 Rolling Stone story “The Great American Bubble Machine” called Goldman Sachs “a great vampire squid wrapped around the face of humanity.” The line stuck well enough that then-Goldman CEO Lloyd Blankfein conceded in August of that year that the article had “tapped into something.” Economists got their own monster: zombie firms. Bank for International Settlements researchers Ryan Banerjee and Boris Hofmann define them as companies at least 10 years old whose earnings haven’t covered their interest payments for three straight years. Across 14 advanced economies, the zombie share of listed non-financial companies rose from 4% in the late 1980s to 15% in 2017. The researchers found that weak banks that roll over loans instead of writing them off keep zombie companies on life support, and that lower interest rates tend to push the zombie share up. The newest candidates are in software. PitchBook counted $131 billion of leveraged loans priced below 80 cents on the dollar, a common threshold for distress and the most since May 2020. Software companies account for 32% of those distressed loans while making up just 12% of the index, and 84% of stressed software companies are backed by private equity. These are castles that can’t pay their own upkeep, kept standing by somebody else’s money, the dollar-princess arrangement with a credit line instead of a dowry. The anxiety is big business for the haunted boom of 2026. Book publisher Zando launched a horror imprint, Evil Twin, planning six to eight titles a year. In Hollywood, Warner Bros.’ R-rated body-horror film Clayface opened No. 1 the weekend of Oct. 23 with about $24 million, according to The Numbers. The machines are writing the stuff now, too. Inkfluence AI, a fiction-writing platform, says 3,929 horror books were started on it in the 180 days to Sept. 12, with haunted-house and ghost premises leading at about a third. Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network
2026-10-30

Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Alongside Norway, it is Ireland’s first time being included in the pan-European network. Ireland has joined the European Laboratory for Learning and Intelligent Systems (ELLIS) network after officially launching ELLIS Unit Ireland. The new unit marks the first time Ireland has joined the network, as ELLIS welcomed other new units in Austria, France, Germany and fellow first-timer Norway. The network has 49 units across Europe and aims to propel the continent as a leader at the forefront of AI research. The units operate as localised hubs whereby researchers can work together, share ideas across a range of disciplines and carry out long-term research. The unit is part of Rinn AI, which is Ireland’s national centre for research and innovation in data science and AI and is funded by Research Ireland. Prof Noel O’Connor, Rinn AI’s director, will lead ELLIS Unit Ireland, which will focus on advancing fundamental AI research in core machine learning and related areas such as computer vision, natural language processing and information retrieval. The unit will also work closely with domain experts, policymakers and societal stakeholders, as a means of addressing real-world challenges. There will be a focus on connecting researchers across the country and training the next generation of ML and AI researchers. Commenting on Ireland’s inclusion to the network, O’Connor said: “Joining ELLIS is a significant moment for AI research in Ireland. Through Rinn AI, we have brought together researchers from every university in the country and ELLIS Unit Ireland now connects that community directly to Europe’s leading network in machine learning. “It opens new doors for collaboration, gives our researchers and students a platform on the European stage and helps ensure the AI we develop is shaped by the people it serves.” SiliconRepublic.com recently spoke with Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork, about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares
2026-10-30

Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
University of Bristol’s Prof Michelle Spear looks at how your brain could be responsible for things that go bump in the night, not ghosts. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) It’s three in the morning. You’ve woken from a dead sleep because you’ve heard a thud in the darkness or a floorboard creaking downstairs. Or perhaps you’ve woken because you have the unmistakable sensation you’re not alone. During daylight, these phenomena might be dismissed as the heating, your neighbours or the sounds of an old house settling. But in the middle of the night, they can feel a tad more sinister. Your mind might immediately start reeling, jumping to the worst-case scenario – wondering if you’ve been broken into, or if you’re being haunted by some supernatural force. In reality, the explanation is far more simple. Things really do go bump in the night. But occasionally, the culprit is closer to home than we might imagine. Falling asleep is not the neurological equivalent of switching off a light. The brain moves gradually from wakefulness into sleep, accompanied by changes in sensory processing, muscle activity and awareness of the world around us. But these changes don’t always occur in perfect synchrony. So for brief periods, characteristics of sleep and wakefulness can overlap. The results can be surprisingly spooky. If you’ve ever been on the verge of sleep only to feel that you’ve fallen off a kerb, missed a step or dropped through the bed, you’ve probably experienced a hypnic jerk. Also known as a sleep start, this is a sudden involuntary contraction of one or more muscles as we move into the earliest stages of sleep. It’s an example of how the transition between wakefulness and sleep isn’t always a smooth one. Hypnagogic experiences can also involve vivid sounds, images or sensations. So as you’re on the edge of sleep, you may think you hear footsteps or whispers – or you may even think you see a figure in the room. Exploding head syndrome – perhaps the most alarming name for a harmless sleep condition – is another example of how the boundaries between sleep and wakefulness can become blurred. As someone is falling asleep, or occasionally waking, they suddenly perceive an enormous noise: an explosion, gunshot or slamming door – yet nothing in the outside world has made a sound. Its precise cause remains uncertain. But the phenomenon demonstrates something important: hearing a sound and a sound actually occurring are not quite the same thing. The conscious experience of hearing is constructed by the brain. So neural activity generated without an external sound can still produce an auditory experience that seems entirely real. The brain doesn’t even need to be asleep for this to happen. If you’ve ever thought you’ve heard someone call your name – only to turn around and find no one there – you’ve been tricked by your brain. Another possible explanation for the sounds we think we hear at night involves source monitoring. This is the brain’s ability to distinguish events generated internally from those coming from the outside world. Thoughts, memories and imagined speech are internally generated, yet normally retain the quality of being ours. But occasionally, that attribution goes awry – allowing something generated within the brain to feel as though it came from somewhere else. Around sleep, that boundary becomes particularly interesting. Another common – but frightening – experience that can happen at night is waking up with the sudden feeling that someone else is in the room. This experience is usually the result of sleep paralysis. During rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, when most of our vivid dreaming occurs, the brain temporarily suppresses activity in most muscles. This protective mechanism, known as REM atonia, prevents us from physically acting out our dreams. But during sleep paralysis, our waking awareness returns while this physical paralysis persists. You know where you are, but cannot move. Yet elements of dream perception can remain active. This is why you may have experienced the sensation that someone – or something – is in the room with you, despite clear sensory evidence of the contrary. One clue as to why this happens comes from experiments examining how the brain distinguishes itself from others. In one study, participants controlled a robotic device positioned in front of them, while a second robot, which was placed behind them, reproduced these movements by touching the participants’ backs. But when researchers introduced a slight delay between the movement and the touch, some participants began to feel as though somebody else was touching them. Some even sensed another person standing behind them. While this experiment doesn’t explain every felt presence that can happen during sleep paralysis, it does reveal something remarkable: the distinction between ourselves and others is something the brain has to construct. Usually, movement, touch and our sense of body position agree about what belongs to us. But if that relationship is disrupted, an internally generated sensation can begin to feel like it originated elsewhere. Now picture this happening in a dark bedroom, when you’re partly awake yet still on the edge of dreaming and unable to move. The sensation that somebody else is there becomes rather less mysterious. Perception is not a passive recording of the world. It combines incoming sensory information with expectation and previous experience to construct the most likely explanation. But at the boundary between sleep and waking, this process can become less reliable. We may hear sounds that were never made, experience fragments of dreams intruding our bedroom or interpret sensations arising from our own bodies as the presence of somebody else. Michelle Spear is a professor of anatomy at the University of Bristol. Her research focuses on the architecture of synovial joints, drawing on her experience in histopathology to examine clinical and functional aspects of tissue-based research. Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland
2026-10-30

Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Prof Paul Townsend tells us about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Over the summer, seven new Research Ireland centres were launched to address key research areas deemed pertinent to the country’s scientific, medical, economic and technological development. The centres belong to a national research network called ‘Rinn’ and between them are set to receive €460m in Government investments over the next eight years, supporting 577 research positions, developing more than 800 PhDs and collaborating with 17 research-performing organisations. The seven Rinn research centres – dedicated to the fields of advanced therapies, AI, energy, medical devices, pharma and biopharma, quantum, and semiconductors – began their work in July, with each being led by directors who are experts in their sectors. Rinn Semiconductors is set to receive nearly €71m of Government funding overall and is headed by Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork (UCC) and with Tyndall National Institute who was born and grew up in the UK. “I studied physics at university, gaining a PhD from the University of Cambridge on the novel properties of polymers that behave as one-dimensional semiconductors,” he tells SiliconRepublic.com. His subsequent work in industry included time spent at British Telecom on quantum cryptography, the then-emerging concept of exploiting quantum physics to secure information. This work, he explains, was ancestral to “the quantum information and computing revolution that we frequently read about” today. “In 2003, I had the opportunity to move to Ireland with my family and colleagues to found a new group at UCC and Tyndall, working on optical fibre communication systems with funding from Science Foundation Ireland,” says Townsend. Tasked then with supporting the development of the wider photonics research ecosystem and engagement with industry in Ireland, this has since become the focus of his time working with Tyndall as its head of photonics research. “My ambition is to take this an exciting further step forward with Rinn Semiconductors,” says Townsend. The centre is set to focus on research and innovation in heterogeneously integrated semiconductor systems, and aims to lead Ireland’s response to the point at which traditional improvements in computer chips diminishes (the end of Moore’s Law scaling) by pioneering modular integrated circuit-based – or chiplet-based – semiconductor design and integration. Chiplet architecture is a method of using multiple smaller modules to build a single larger processor for a more powerful end result. The Rinn Semiconductors team will seek transformative advances in these technologies and to apply them in three main areas: future internet, sustainable energy and the environment, and digital healthcare. “Today, semiconductor chips touch nearly every aspect of our daily lives; they are in phones, computers and medical technology,” notes Townsend. “Almost any problem you can think of – societal or technological – requires the use of semiconductors to provide a solution. “This is also a technology where Ireland plays a leading role. We are home to a world-class semiconductor industry sector – with a 50-year operating history – that employs 20,000 people in more than 100 companies. “The national semiconductor strategy, Silicon Island, aims to build upon this to position Ireland internationally as a hub of semiconductor excellence and to contribute to the EU Chips Act goal of growing the EU’s proportion of global semiconductor manufacturing output.” Key elements of the Government’s Silicon Island strategy, he notes, include strengthening research capacity, supporting start-ups and spin-outs, and building a future-ready talent pipeline through education and skills programmes. “Rinn Semiconductors is extremely well-positioned to make major contributions to the achievement of these goals,” he says. Another “key objective” of the centre, according to Townsend, is “to foster deep collaboration across the seven universities and institutions involved” by leveraging the “world-class” semiconductor fabrication facilities and infrastructure at Tyndall. The centre plans for what it calls “radical collaboration” with and between Dublin City University, Munster Technological University, Trinity College Dublin, Tyndall, UCC, University of Galway and University of Limerick. This, it is hoped, will lead to “a cohesive ‘atom to systems’ value chain in Ireland, connecting everything from basic materials research through to complete technology solutions”, says Townsend. The Rinn Network, in his view, offers the opportunity to leverage coordinated research at scale, which is “critical” to the development of complex technologies such as semiconductors. “The significant scale of the industry collaborative research projects we have established in just the first two months of operation gives me great confidence that this vision can be realised.” As centre director, Townsend is responsible for ensuring that his programme is “suitably prioritised, innovative, impactful, internationally leading in its focus areas and, critically, delivers on its mission and goals”. He is also in charge of budgeting, and overseeing grant compliance and reporting. “In addition, I take the lead on stakeholder and external relations management, and act as an advocate for the centre and its mission,” he says. Another important part of his leadership role is “to ensure the centre is a beacon for attracting and developing talent at all levels from PhD students to senior principal investigators (PIs)” in order to “address the demand from industry for highly-skilled scientists and engineers, as well as to sustain the academic research ecosystem needed to supply these trainees and keep Ireland at the forefront of semiconductor research”. Apart from the development and implementation of the centre’s scientific and operational strategy, Townsend – whose personal research background is in optical fibre communications and sensing – gets some hands-on time at Rinn Semiconductors, too. “I am also an active PI in the centre, where my group focuses on developing new integrated semiconductor solutions for two main application areas: energy-efficient interconnect technologies for AI clusters in data centres, with the goal of constraining the overall power consumption of AI; and distributed acoustic sensing in optical fibre cables, where we target improved monitoring of undersea communication cables and offshore windfarm power cables to ensure resilience and protection.” Moving forward, Townsend says his Rinn centre is targeting progress in four main areas: research breakthroughs in semiconductor chiplet integration and packaging technologies; translation of these ideas into new manufacturable technologies and products; acting as a catalyst for talent development; and driving engaged research programmes with stakeholders for impact on policy and society. The latter, he hopes, could include “addressing the early stages of the talent supply problem by working with teachers and policymakers on how to encourage more school and university students, particularly women, to take up physics and engineering subjects that can lead to a career in the semiconductor industry”. Townsend notes, too, the potential of the overall network for fostering collaboration and shared progress across the seven cornerstone areas, and the prospect of creating “greater value and impact than could be achieved by a group of individual centres working in a less connected way”. “The wider Rinn Network plan adds the exciting possibility for centres to work together on complex problems that can only be addressed through an interdisciplinary approach. “I think the scale and ambition of the overall programme will also be a great attractor for external talent and a fantastic springboard for researchers already in Ireland to advance their careers. An especially important goal to him on an individual level, he adds, would be “the development of the next generation of exciting, talented researchers who will take Ireland on to even greater success in the future”. “I am very fortunate and proud to have had the opportunity to work in Ireland and to contribute at least somewhat to the development of the national research ecosystem. “On the personal side, my wife and I and our two now-grown-up daughters all live happily in and around Cork – and wouldn’t want to be anywhere else.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Soaring memory prices – driven up by the continued unprecedented demand for AI chips – has had a notable negative impact across the consumer devices market, including personal computers. The report also forecasts a 27pc year-over-year drop in sales for the upcoming quarter. “Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,” noted Intel chief financial officer David Zinsner. “We remain optimistic about the opportunities across our broader portfolio, and we will continue making disciplined investments in capacity and working capital to increase supply and support future growth.” Just days ago, Intel announced plans to purchase Israeli quantum company Classiq to create a full-stack quantum computing platform. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal
2026-10-30

Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Jocelyn Bell Burnell. Image: Chris Bellew, Fennell Photography The astrophysicist has a long legacy of scientific research, discovery and supporting the wider STEM community. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell has been awarded the UCD Ulysses Medal for her outstanding scientific achievements and ongoing commitment to developing the next generation of researchers. The Ulysses Medal is awarded to those who have contributed something of immense importance on a global scale. Previous recipients include author Margaret Atwood, former Irish President Mary McAleese and the ‘Godfather of AI’, Prof Geoffrey Hinton. Raised in Northern Ireland, Burnell previously described her early STEM education as challenging. Despite her assurance that she wanted to study the stars surrounding our planet, her gender precluded her from engaging with STEM subjects in school, as they were aimed primarily at young men. She persevered, becoming one of only three young girls taking science in her secondary school, where she topped the class in physics and then continued on to the University of Glasgow and then Cambridge, where she undertook a PhD. It was at Cambridge where she worked on the development of a radio space telescope designed to explore the furthest reaches of space and where she discovered a “scruff” on one of the charts she was analysing. This was the moment she identified what would become known as a pulsating radio star, or pulsar. Despite her work however, Burnell was notably excluded when the discovery was recognised by the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1974. The prize was instead awarded to her PhD supervisor, Antony Hewish and a fellow Cambridge professor, Martin Ryle. Her exclusion stands as a prime example of how the achievements of women were so often disregarded in favour of their male co-workers and supervisors. In 2018, she donated £2.3m to start the Bell Burnell Graduate Scholarship Fund, a fund supporting students coming from underrepresented backgrounds. The money was part of the Special Breakthrough Prize in Fundamental Physics that she had earned for her previous discovery and five decades of leadership in the field. Prof Lorraine Hanlon , a full professor of astronomy at UCD, said Burnell had been “peerless in how she has deployed her brilliance, transforming her groundbreaking discovery into lifelong advocacy for curiosity-driven research, equity and global science diplomacy. “The achievements of all the Bell Burnell scholars stand as living proof of Dame Jocelyn’s enduring legacy in recognising and empowering the next generation of scientific leaders.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Psychedelic Compound in Toad Venom Can Treat Depression When Snorted, Scientists Find
2026-10-31

Psychedelic Compound in Toad Venom Can Treat Depression When Snorted, Scientists Find

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
In the late 1980s and early 90s, sensational reports of a “toad licking” fad spread like wildfire throughout North and South America. The stories — aided in no small part by harrowing advisories from the US Drug Enforcement Administration — told of an epidemic of teens licking toads in order to ingest compounds secreted by the animals, most notably bufotenine, an alkaloid compound with psychedelic effects. How widespread this phenomenon actually was in practice is difficult to say. Contemporary media analysis found that tales of rampant toad-tonguing spread through a game of academic telephone, which was quickly picked up by mainstream media. As all media frenzies do, the supposed drug epidemic quickly fell off the radar, and psychedelic toads became something of an obscure proverb known mainly to drug researchers. Decades later, however, ongoing research into the substances secreted by the amphibians seems to have led to an actual breakthrough, in the form of a single-dose nasal spray meant to treat severe cases of depression. As ScienceAlert reports, researchers at the Eli Lilly subsidiary AtaiBeckley recently concluded clinical trials around mebufotenin, one of the active psychedelic compounds found in the venom of the Sonoran Desert toad. Per their findings, published in the Journal of the American Medical Association, doses of mebufotenin spray demonstrated “potential as an effective, well-tolerated therapy for treatment-resistant depression with a rapid recovery time.” The findings come via a double-blind randomized clinical trial involving 193 adults with treatment-resistant depression, which is characterized by the Cleveland Clinic as major depressive disorder which persists following at least two different antidepressant treatments. Overall, the researchers found that eight and 12 milligram doses of toad spray resulted in significantly reduced scores on a standard depression test, with only mild to moderate side-effects. Even more promising is the fact that those positive effects held for weeks after a single dose. “Single-day dosing with mebufotenin appears to be effective for postpartum depression — and now for difficult-to-treat depression — and the benefits are sustained with or without re-treatment for eight days to six months,” psychopharmacology researcher Chittaranjan Andrade told ScienceAlert. Still, Andrade isn’t convinced that toad secretions are the holy grail of antidepressants, at least based on this clinical trial, which — while being the largest study yet of mebufotenin as a nasal spray — still has key limitations. “The average patient became only about 30 percent better,” Andrade explained in his own editorial, “and the response and remission rates were below those typically seen, for example, with ketamine and electroconvulsive therapy.” “Patients with difficult-to-treat depression require long-term maintenance treatment,” Andrade continued, “but because mebufotenin was examined as monotherapy, the trial offers no insight on when and how the transition to a conventional antidepressant should be made or whether mebufotenin itself might be used for maintenance.” More on treatments: Chinese Scientists Develop Muscle Grafts That Can Be Injected Straight Under the Skin

Creeps Are Using AI to Undress Celebrities in Paparazzi Shots for Clicks

Engagement hackers on X-formerly-Twitter have a new strategy to attract attention: use AI to undress celebrity men and publish it as if it’s the real deal. The strategy, which was flagged earlier this week by writer Sarah Hagi, is pretty simple. Posters take existing paparazzi photos of celebrity men and alter them so that the men are shirtless and, most often, extremely jacked. They then republish the synthetic images as if they’re new and real. The undressing is more than likely nonconsensual, making the whole scheme pretty gross. One frequent target is Callum Turner, the British actor and husband of pop star Dua Lipa. An account called “UKViewTIme” has posted several different AI-altered photos of Turner and Lipa together on the streets of NYC. Lipa, for her part, is seen completely clothed in pants and long jackets — while Turner is unnecessarily strolling about shirtless for no clear reason. Original paparazzi shots show that Turner was clothed in real life. Another recent post, published by an account called “BiTheWay,” show images of the “Stranger Things” star Noah Schnapp walking with the actor Sheridan Townsley. In the altered image published by “BiTheWay,” both of the young men are inexplicably pictured shirtless, however the original paparazzi images reveal that both Schnapp and Townsley were indeed wearing shirts and jackets. The culprits are going to extreme lengths to preserve the alleged authenticity of their inspiration. For instance, they’re going as far as to ensure that pop sensation Harry Styles’ famous tattoos are intact in AI-manipulated, topless images. “UKViewTime” was the culprit once again. Another repeat target has been Knox Jolie-Pitt, the son of Angelina Jolie and Brad Pitt. We also found one manipulated image of Pitt himself, who, despite wearing a yellow suit in the original photos, was altered to be shown in a black wife beater that reveals artificially-jacked arms. The deepfakes can be hard to spot because most of the images are in large part real. But if you find yourself wondering why Dua Lipa is dressed in an ankle-sweeping coat while her husband is out sans shirt, the answer is likely because you’re looking at a reality skewed by AI. After Uber Destroyed Taxi Cabs, Drivers Now Face the Existential Threat of Self-Driving Cars
2026-10-31

After Uber Destroyed Taxi Cabs, Drivers Now Face the Existential Threat of Self-Driving Cars

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
In its first decade of existence, Uber managed to completely upend the decades-old taxi industry, shredding through million-dollar yellow cab dynasties and owner-operators alike. Now that those erstwhile cabbies have come to reluctant terms with the new algorithmic paradigm, self-driving cars are exploding onto the scene — pushing the workers who move America to their breaking point. In Cambridge, Massachusetts, a coalition of worker advocacy groups and unions have come together to warn local lawmakers about the threat to their livelihoods, and to demand that the city take action to preserve drivers’ jobs. According to Cambridge Daily, the group was made up of a newly organized App Drivers Union, which was joined by transit workers from Boston Carmen’s Union Local 589, and transportation and logistics workers from Teamster’s Local 25. They gathered in response to the rising presence of Waymo vehicles on Cambridge streets, which have begun mapping the roadways in obvious preparation for an eventual rollout of autonomous vehicles. “I used to drive a cab, but lost my business to Uber,” said Abdazi Samantar, a local rideshare driver whose livelihood is now threatened by robotaxi companies like Waymo. “It feels like every time I adjust to a new reality, another technology comes along and threatens my livelihood.” Whether Waymos literally takeover the role of rideshare drivers is maybe beside the point. Like the impact of apps like Uber and Lyft on cab drivers, robotaxis have a depressive effect on driver pay, leading to a gradual lessening of wages as competition for riders increases. A case in point is Atlanta, where the local Rideshare Drivers Union has noted that workers have received lower pay, longer down-time between rides, and fewer overall fares since Waymo came to town in summer of 2025. Though state officials across the US have mostly declined to allow municipal governments to enact labor protections for workers from rapidly-expanding robotaxi companies — let alone from exploitation by app giants like Uber — organizers in Atlanta have built a strong movement around practical economic demands. For example, the ARDU has proposed a “robotaxi impact fee” of between $0.50 and $1.00 per ride, to be paid into a “driver transition fund” that would then offer alternative job training and financial assistance to struggling drivers. Reacting to driver demands, rideshare giants like Uber have come out in favor of limiting autonomous vehicles, putting additional pressure on local lawmakers to protect worker’s rights. Whether rideshare workers in Cambridge can secure similar concessions remains to be seen, but the threat grows more acute with every street Waymo maps — meaning time is of the essence. Investor Says We’re Rapidly Approaching the Point At Which Economic Systems Collapse Under the Weight of AI
2026-10-31

Investor Says We’re Rapidly Approaching the Point At Which Economic Systems Collapse Under the Weight of AI

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
At this point, the parade of doomsayers warning of the collapse of the AI bubble is almost endless. Renowned financial commentators like Harris Kupperman have warned that the math on data center investments is unsustainable, economists like Michael Roberts have called AI the “biggest economic bet in US history,” and even the International Monetary Fund has begun fretting that the world economy is on a knife’s edge. But in the ongoing AI gold rush, the wealthy and powerful keep pumping money into AI and its attendant industries anyway. Now, crypto investor and finance media guru Raoul Pal is warning of his own kind of AI doomsday — but one where an AI takeover, not a bursting bubble, brings the conventional economy to its knees. “The next four years are going to feel very different to anything we’ve lived through,” Pal wrote in a post on X-formerly-Twitter. The post is nakedly self-promotional — it appears alongside a clip of the finance pundit’s “When Shift Happens podcast,” and is followed by a link to buy his book, “The Everything Code” — but even so, Pal does raise some bold claims to his 1.5 million followers. “Your wages haven’t kept up with the cost of living for years now,” he exclaims. “Governments have to keep debasing the currency to fund everything, and it’s eating into your pay every single year. That part isn’t new.” “What is new,” Pal continues, “is that we have maybe four years before the world as we know it stops working the way it does now. The exponential technologies… AI, robotics, energy, crypto… are moving so fast that the systems built for the old world can’t keep up.” Eventually, in Pal’s reading, the development of these industries will lead to an economy in which the average person “can’t trust what you see or hear anymore.” “Even the idea of running a business stops meaning what it means today,” he says — a moment he calls the “Economic Singularity.” As the saying goes, strong claims require even stronger evidence. Bold though Pal’s prophecies are, they rest on some vague observations of the world taken as gospel. Persistent inflation, for example — which seems to be the foundation of Pal’s metaphysical theory — is not a new phenomenon, and the crypto investor is far from the first one to observe it. As the economist Richard Wolff observed in 2022, inflationary pressure is a longstanding feature of capitalism, not a portent of end-times. As for whether macroeconomic conditions will be drastically changed four years from now, that may very well be the case, but the same could likely be said for 2004 versus 2008, or 2016 compared to 2020. Those moments of severe economic crises changed things dramatically for working class people, but the structures of capitalism survived. Pal’s arrival as one of many woo woo doomsayers at a time of severe financial hardship for millions isn’t a coincidence — he’s an oracle for people in desperate need of order among economic chaos. More on AI: Top Economist Finds That AI Has Provided “Zero Financial Payoff” to Companies Outside Tech
Futurism
We Were Totally Wrong About How Life on Earth Started, Study Suggests
2026-10-31

We Were Totally Wrong About How Life on Earth Started, Study Suggests

Futurism | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Scientists have long suspected that comets helped kickstart life on Earth by seeding it with organic molecules. But new research may bring the idea to a screeching halt. A team of researchers led by the University of Cambridge found that a comet’s entry into the Earth’s atmosphere is too violent for the molecular building blocks of life to survive, as detailed in a new study published in the journal Scientific Reports. Any stragglers would be vaporized upon impact with the ground. “Historically, scientists thought that comets were important for the origin of life on Earth, because they carried carbon- and nitrogen-bearing molecules, so these giant space snowballs were almost like a ‘get out of jail free card’ that brought all the molecules a planet would need to seed life,” said lead author and Cambridge astronomer Richard Anslow in a statement about the work. “It’s kind of a romantic idea,” he added, “but our results suggest it’s highly unlikely.” Pseudo-panspermia, the idea that life’s building blocks came from space, has gained credence in recent decades. It’s a “romantic idea” because it also suggests that life may be a common occurrence. Astronomers have frequently found organic molecules on comets and asteroids, suggesting they’re abundant throughout the cosmos. Samples taken from one asteroid called Bennu contained 14 of the 20 amino acids that life uses to build proteins and all five of the molecules that make up DNA and RNA. That discovery in particular supported the “RNA world” hypothesis which posits that the earliest forms of life relied on self-replicating RNA molecules before the advent of DNA and proteins. In a previous study, Anslow suggested that comets could be “bouncing” between planets whose orbits are close together, allowing the comets to slow down to a speed — below nine miles per second — that allows them to deliver organic molecules without destroying them. But the new work shows that the likelihood of this working on Earth is extremely slim. In simulations of comet entries, they focused on hydrogen cyanide (HCN), a prebiotic molecule that’s commonly found on solar system comets. What they discovered was that there’s just too many conditions that would need to be perfect for hydrogen cyanide to survive the trip in one piece. “It would really have to be a one-in-a-million occurrence,” Anslow said, “because not only would you need an impact at just the right speed and just the right angle, you would also need everything to happen after that point, in terms of the concentration and the speed at which the chemical origins of life got going.” “We can’t rule out for certain that it happened that way,” he added. “But it seems to be a really rare occurrence — at best, a one-off fluke.” Any HCN molecules that did survive wouldn’t exist in great enough quantities, proividing “an insignificant source of the concentrated feedstock molecules required for prebiotic chemistry,” the researchers wrote in the study. It might be a considerable blow for the theory on Earth, but the researchers suggest that comets could have seeded life-giving molecules on Mars, which has a thinner atmosphere and makes for a smoother entry. “This is not to say it definitely happened on Mars, of course, but suggests it would be unlikely if it happened on Earth and didn’t happen on Mars,” said Anslow. “Or the other, perhaps more likely, alternative, is that it didn’t happen on either planet.” More on biology: Astronomers Spot Mysterious Object Lurking Beyond Pluto

Tech Leaders Kiss the Ring and Refuse to Call It “AI” After Trump Told Them Not to
2026-10-30

Tech Leaders Kiss the Ring and Refuse to Call It “AI” After Trump Told Them Not to

Futurism | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
It turns out the only thing more sycophantic than AI chatbots are the people in charge of building them. After president Donald Trump decreed that artificial intelligence should now be referred to as “Super Intelligence” (caps included), tech and AI CEOs — or should we say “SI” CEOs — have eagerly complied with this absurd rebranding attempt. An analysis by CNBC found that Microsoft, SpaceX, Meta, Tesla, and IBM all used the term “Super Intelligence” or “SI” in official documents and communications this earnings season. One SpaceX filing referred to SI 85 times. Microsoft, in an annual report letter, even heralded the arrival of a “Super Intelligence era.” “For Microsoft, the central question as we enter this Super Intelligence era is not just how big the opportunity becomes, but how we deliver the real-world outcomes that every country, community, and company demands and deserves,” CEO Satya Nadella wrote in the letter. Trump’s obsession with rebranding AI seemed to come out of nowhere. Last month, he launched a poll on his website Truth Social asking his supporters to choose a better name for AI. Days later, he told the UN General Assembly that all US government documents will ditch the term AI in favor of SI, and made the decree official with an executive order. This week, he escalated proceedings by saying he would be “officially BANNING” the term AI in government documents and “everything else.” That tech leaders have embraced using SI encapsulates the extraordinary toadying that they have continued to show Trump since he returned to the Oval Office, including those whom Trump once perceived as an enemy. Perhaps the most enthusiastic adopter of the new moniker is SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk, who remains closely tied to the administration. He has insisted on using the term SI in all his public appearances and posts (with occasional slip-ups), and rebranded his AI company SpaceXAI as SpaceXSI. Recently, Musk even admitted that he has “SI psychosis.” OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, meanwhile, called Super Intelligence a “more accurate” term than artificial intelligence, but shot down the idea of rebranding OpenAI as OpenSI. In general, he has sounded skeptical about the rebrand’s usefulness. “I think people are going to keep calling it AI,” he told Politico. Pile-ins, payouts, and pacing pressure: the investors behind the VCs open up
2026-10-31

Pile-ins, payouts, and pacing pressure: the investors behind the VCs open up

TechCrunch | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. The limited partners who provide the capital to venture firms — the “money behind the money” — are feeling great right now. They’re in uncharted territory in many ways, but also potentially exceedingly lucrative territory. Normally, LPs typically spread their bets across many firms to diversify their own portfolios. They write checks to emerging managers and established ones, to generalist firms and specialists. The goal is a strong return, but also to minimize risk. That’s the typical playbook. But typical is out the window. OpenAI and Anthropic’s valuations have shot up so quickly that nearly everyone has piled into the same two companies on the way up, often inside the same venture firm, despite the longstanding venture convention of avoiding investments that conflict with one another. No one is complaining, either. The LPs I speak with seem unbothered by this big gamble, given the potential returns; they’re expecting a bonanza. But they do see danger ahead, even if they describe this as a “yellow light” situation, and they’re uneasy about how quickly VCs are coming back to raise new funds. I talked about all of it recently with Beezer Clarkson of LGT Capital Partners, a Zurich-based diversified asset manager, and Amit Bhatti of TrueBridge, a Chapel Hill firm that focuses solely on venture. This conversation has been edited for length. These are strange days — exciting but also scary. How are you feeling about your roles right now, and your place in this startup ecosystem? Beezer Clarkson: It’s very busy, but I wouldn’t say it’s scary. There’s a lot of change and a lot of variables at play. I love venture, so I find the chaos fun. I’ve been doing this for a couple of decades, so seeing the cycles makes it less scary. You appreciate that these things happen, and that we should enjoy the upside, because sometimes there’s downside, and the up is a lot more fun than the down. Amit Bhatti: If anything, it’s exciting. Venture is sometimes looked at as a niche, and now that AI is all-consuming, all the attention is on venture. This feels like our moment more than it is like, “Oh gosh, what’s going to happen?” The pace of investing and the valuations may be different, but really meaningful stuff is happening, and all the attention is focused on this corner of private equity. Your funds all hold the same brands now, OpenAI and Anthropic, multiplied across however many funds you back, and your job is to keep your institutions diversified. How do you think about that? Obviously it’s great right now, but if things turned, it would be less great. Amit Bhatti: Venture has always been a power law business, so I actually view the concentration as a good thing. If we were getting to the maturity of one of our funds and there were still lots of tiny positions, something’s gone wrong. Our funds that are 10-plus years old are starting to consolidate into a few names. There’s a lot of Databricks, a lot of SpaceX, a lot of Stripe. In this generation of funds, I would hope there’s a lot of Anthropic and a lot of OpenAI. That’s a feature, not a bug. Not every one of these companies is going to work out, but that consolidation is typical. What’s different is how quickly it’s happened. I don’t view it as an immediate panic signal. Beezer Clarkson: At the early stage, you still see quite a diversity of opportunity sets, and the dollars are smaller. It aggregates up into the same companies, but early-stage funds obviously aren’t doing the big late-stage stuff, so you have more diversity there, if that’s what you’re looking for. Funds that invested on four-year cycles have shrunk that to three, then two, then 18 months. Is the pacing okay, or is it concerning? Beezer Clarkson: We’re seeing funds come back on one-year pacing, which is fast. The norm was probably two to two and a half years. Some funds are deploying much faster because they’re taking concentrated, big-check positions. That could be a worrisome sign. It could work out, we don’t know, but it’s typically harder to do a one-year vintage and get it right. Amit Bhatti: Similar for us. I would have said 24 to 36 months was roughly normal. Now 12 to 18 is not crazy for some of the funds going really quickly. A lot of funds are bumping up their fund sizes mid-cycle, which is definitely unusual. That’s partly because rounds have gotten so large. If Anthropic is raising a giant quantum of capital and you put 20% of the fund into it, you’re obviously going to be ahead of pace. Seed round sizes have gone up almost 2x, too, so if you want to lead, you’re writing 2x the check. It’s definitely a yellow light. It gives us a little bit of heartburn. But when we look at what these managers actually bought, the numbers are different. The last time we had accelerated pacing, there was a lot of investing in zero-revenue crypto moonshots. Now we’re seeing a lot of companies go from zero to $100 million in revenue, which was totally unheard of before. If you’ve got a great manager who comes back every year but the returns are still on paper, can you say “call me next time,” or do you lose your seat at the table? Beezer Clarkson: It’s very hard to leave a manager and then come back. It happens, but it’s more typical to downsize your check. In 2021, a lot of LPs said, “If I was going to give you $10 million, I’ll do $5 million, because $10 million was for two years.” In our portfolios, the pacing gets pulled through, so we think about our pacing as well as yours. Amit Bhatti: Same here. We’re building out a calendar of when we expect to make commitments, so if someone goes way off that map, we need to make sure the capital is there. You see people downsize or communicate it up front. It’s hard to say you’re sitting it out altogether, because then you’re not reliable capital. That’s also why you’re seeing upsizes; managers don’t want to come back and say they’re raising a whole new fund, so they bump up the old one. Beezer Clarkson: People also rotate out of venture sometimes because capital calls keep running with no distributions coming back, and they still have to meet operating commitments, whether it’s the endowment, the hospital, or the foundation. That puts a lot of strain on those situations. LPs like time diversification, yet a lot of funds were raised in recent years, including that backed startups at high valuations during the pandemic. How are those funds doing, and how would you describe 2026 so far? Beezer Clarkson: The 2021 vintages are still early, five years in, but they argue against doing one-year funds. Some will catch a piece of an AI company and look excellent, but your odds of getting it right in one year are just tougher, and the valuations were extreme. Amit Bhatti: If I look at the last 15 years of our portfolio, I wouldn’t bet on the COVID vintage being the best one. But a couple of breakouts will come from it. OpenAI was around in that era, and Cursor started pretty far back, so they’re in some of those funds. And liquidity has started flowing. The past two years were definitely slower, but SpaceX is the headline, Cerebras was a big exit this year, there have been a couple of other IPOs, and a lot of M&A. We’re seeing $10 billion-plus M&A exits, a $60 billion one [with Cursor]. Liquidity is going beyond just IPOs. How are your venture fund managers thinking about secondaries? If I were a VC, I’d have been tempted to take money off the table in OpenAI or Anthropic. Beezer Clarkson: Secondaries have become much more sophisticated in venture over the last, call it, five years. It’s localized in the top handful of companies, but it’s much more prevalent than before, which I think is good, because the ecosystem needs liquidity options. Venture is predicated on that, and if you wait 20 years for an IPO, that is not what structurally we all thought it could be. They’re also happening at multiple levels now. LPs sell stakes of their GPs, a strip of their portfolio, and the taboo around it is less. Amit Bhatti: It’s the third leg of liquidity now: IPOs, M&A, secondaries. Private equity has always had a well-developed secondaries market, and venture didn’t. It was kind of a dirty word. You were only selling if you were distressed. Then people were holding the bag in 2021 and thought, “I should have sold.” Now more managers use it as a tool, and more judiciously. If you still believe it’s the power law company, you’re probably not exiting the whole position. But if you can sell 20% of your position and return your fund, you should probably do it. There’s a lot of founder selling and employee tenders — often very early into the company’s “journey.” How do you feel about that? Amit Bhatti: We were talking before about the analogy to crypto, where things got very difficult in part because employees were paid in tokens that were liquid immediately. What’s your motivation to stick around when things get hard? So I think it’s great that there are employee tenders, especially for companies that have been around a long time, like Stripe and Databricks, with a very clear financial picture. For them to offer partial liquidity to employees is totally reasonable. But a lot of the enforcement falls on the lead investors. Investors we back have told companies, “We don’t want you to do an employee tender right now.” Those conversations are happening behind the scenes, and people have learned some lessons from last time. It’s all based on leverage, though. If the founder has incredible leverage . . . When these companies go public and employees get liquid, will they become partners or competitors to your venture managers? These employees have amazing networks. Beezer Clarkson: We’ve seen it multiple times: when a company becomes very productive, employees can become LPs or GPs themselves. It’s entirely possible there’ll be a whole new raft of emerging managers backed by people coming out of OpenAI and Anthropic. It happened in the Google days. But being an LP means signing up for many millions of dollars and a multi-decade business. Amit Bhatti: The real estate part [given the soaring cost of home prices in San Francisco] is going to be tough. But those people stay in the ecosystem, investing in others, building new companies, starting new firms. That’s why no other place is taking SF’s place anytime soon. Housing costs here in San Francisco have shot up so fast. Do we price out the next generation of founders, and does the next hub end up in New York or somewhere else? Beezer Clarkson: History would argue that the density effect in the Bay Area is a great pull. Maybe it moves out of San Francisco and back into the suburbs. Back in the day, YC was in Mountain View. New York obviously has its own very deep tech scene, but there’s such a density here that it’s been really hard to push back against. A handful of venture funds now lock down most of the committed capital from limited partners. Is there a wedge in for emerging managers? Beezer Clarkson: There are still first-time and emerging managers, funds one through three, being raised this year, last year, the year before. You still need a differentiated point of view, a reason for being. Fundraising has gotten harder, but we see them being funded, and the fund sizes are just smaller. Fifty percent of the money might go to the big funds, but if your average first fund is $25 million to $50 million, there can still be enough of those to add up to $1 billion. Amit Bhatti: There are people raising $5 million or $10 million right here in SF, maybe formalizing their angel investing. A lot of really well-known funds started with a $5 million fund. It’s much better for an emerging manager to build the track record, prove out the thesis, your access and your judgment with whatever capital you have, and then fund two will be easier to raise. If you try to raise $50 million or $100 million on day one, you’re creating a giant hill to climb. Beezer Clarkson: If you’ve only angel invested, that’s a different story from spinning out of a platform where you have an installed base of LPs who know you. Those managers typically raise more. Can you talk about specific kinds of emerging managers you’ve backed, and what made you write the check? Amit Bhatti: People define “emerging” very differently. We’ve backed founders who are still running their companies and investing on the side. We’ve backed people leaving a bigger firm for something earlier. And we’ve backed extremely young GPs, 22 and 23 years old, who have really close access to the next generation of AI-native founders. They started an accelerator program at MIT, Harvard, and Stanford, and the first couple of batches had Cursor and Mercor in them. They’re young, but they’re around an incredible density of talent. The VC job is sourcing, picking, winning, and they did a pretty decent job of picking when there was no money involved. Success begets success in venture, so we were happy to give them a shot. Beezer Clarkson: There’s much more flexibility now. I remember a time when an LP wouldn’t fund somebody who had a job and was also investing, and people are open to that now. I’ve been investing in emerging managers, along with established ones, for 14 years, and they run the whole gamut, from an operator-turned-investor raising $10 million, to a spinout raising a few hundred million [dollars]. It’s interesting when they’re still operating, because it gives them insights. The trick is whether they’re doing what they need to do to win the right to be on the best cap tables. Amit Bhatti: Especially early stage, there’s no one-size-fits-all. You can be part-time, you can be diversified, you can be concentrated. There are more outs in the deck, if you will, for returning something that’s small and experimental. What’s the smallest check you’ll write to a fund, and how much of a fund will you take? Beezer Clarkson: Given our program, it’s hard to do less than $5 million. I’ve generally taken the view that if you’re [representing] too much of a fund, there are challenges for the GP going forward, so I’d want to be no more than 20%. There are situations where I’d consider more. Amit Bhatti: Three to five million for us, and on occasion smaller in very specific scenarios. We don’t really care about concentration if it’s the right scenario. We’ll do half or all of it. But Beezer’s right that it’s not ideal for the GP, and we tell them up front to diversify their LP base. We’ve done a fund where we were half the capital, and the next fund we were closer to 20%, because they did well. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Nvidia weighs playing the cosigner for Nscale’s AI data center in West Virginia
