Exclusive details about Akai’s MPC Sample Key
2026-10-30

Exclusive details about Akai’s MPC Sample Key

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
The open-source MPC Sample Key won’t be focused on chopping up drum breaks. The open-source MPC Sample Key won’t be focused on chopping up drum breaks. Akai teased its new MPC Sample Key on Instagram earlier this week, and said it plans to open-source its software so the modding community can hack it to their hearts’ delight. While there’s no word yet on when the device will launch or how much it will cost, The Verge has received exclusive images of the upcoming instrument that offer some details about its feature set. While the Sample Key shares many of the same controls as the MPC Sample — including the three context-sensitive encoders, a large main encoder — it seems designed for pitched sample playback, rather than chopping up drums. In fact, the “chop” button from the MPC Sample is missing entirely from the Sample Key. The dedicated pitch and mod wheels are another clue there. But there are also buttons for changing the octave range of the 25-key keyboard, as well as ones labeled “chord” and “arp.” Presumably, those enable a chord playback mode and an arpeggiator. While using an arpeggiator to play back drum samples can be a fun way to create rhythms, it’s more commonly used for melodic and bass parts. And a chord mode would be basically useless for drums. Those options also appear to replace the chop function from the standard MPC Sample. There also appears to be a dedicated looper that could be useful for live performance and layering sounds. And, instead of the Flex Beat effects from the original MPC Sample, there’s something called “Swarm.” Though what that is isn’t exactly clear at the moment. Like the first entry in the Sample series, the Key has a built-in mic and speaker for all-in-one music production. While we haven’t gotten a glimpse at the back panel yet, the config options on the front suggest that, in addition to MIDI, the Sample Key will have CV outs for integrating with modular synths. Correction October 30th: The MPC Sample Key is 25 keys, not 37. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.
The Verge
Sam Altman says it’s better for RAM to be in a data center than on your desk
2026-10-30

Sam Altman says it’s better for RAM to be in a data center than on your desk

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
As OpenAI’s buying spree squeezes the world’s supply of RAM, its CEO explains why he needs so much.﻿ As OpenAI’s buying spree squeezes the world’s supply of RAM, its CEO explains why he needs so much.﻿ Asked about supply chain shortages, like the ongoing RAM shortage, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said in an interview that it’s “terrible” to have RAM in personal PCs instead of data centers. During his appearance on the Cheeky Pint podcast, where he also commented on AI’s impact on math, Altman said: “A lot of people want local inference. And somehow, they assume that the problem is solved now because we are going to have open-source models, we’re going to be able to run them on our computers, and so we’re not going to need all the stuff in the data center. But from a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used five percent of the time. You want maximum utilization in the cloud if you’re trying to deal with this very supply constrained environment. And somehow everyone’s— many people’s intuition is the opposite of that.” Whether Altman meant your gaming PC or developers working with something like the Surface RTX Spark Dev Box or the new touchscreen MacBook Pro, the outcome is the same. Right now, memory companies Samsung and SK Hynix have long term deals with cloud service operators and projects like OpenAI’s Stargate that have reserved much of the world’s RAM output over the next few years. And the billions they’re willing to lay out for chips is why the laptops, phones, and other tech you use are getting more expensive right now. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The biggest names in AI are backing a new way to opt out of scraping
2026-10-30

The biggest names in AI are backing a new way to opt out of scraping

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
The new control will allow publishers to apply their AI training preferences across multiple crawlers. The new control will allow publishers to apply their AI training preferences across multiple crawlers. OpenAI, Google, Microsoft, and Anthropic say they will adopt a new standard that publishers can use to opt out of being scraped by AI, as reported earlier by Search Engine Roundtable. With this control, publishers can apply their AI training preferences across multiple AI companies, instead of setting them individually. The shared “policy token” signal is proposed by Kevin Kelley, a technical lead for standards at Anthropic, as part of the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF). It will live within a website’s robots.txt file, which has long signaled which web crawlers can and can’t scrape a site — whether for search indexing, archiving, or AI training. Currently, sites have to signal their AI training preferences separately to each AI company. The announcement comes as publishers grapple with the continuing influx of AI crawlers scraping their sites and feeding their content into AI models. Some companies and organizations have attempted to combat unauthorized scraping, with the Really Simple Licensing (RSL) standard giving publishers the ability to charge crawlers that visit their sites, and Cloudflare blocking AI crawlers by default. OpenAI, Google, Microsoft, and Anthropic say they will restrict their AI crawlers from accessing a site if they encounter the shared token. Meta communications head Andy Stone also signaled support for the new setting, but doesn’t say whether the company will honor it. “We believe creating a common signal that conveys such preferences to multiple providers at once is an important step,” Stone says. “And we look forward to working with other AI companies on the standards and their implementation across the industry.” Microsoft says it will offer granular controls that will give publishers the ability to opt out of AI training or AI grounding, the process of pointing an AI model to a verified source of information to reduce hallucinations. Microsoft notes that websites will still appear in Bing’s search results regardless of their preferences. Google previously implemented similar controls that allow sites to control whether their content appears in AI search features or is used for AI training, without impacting visibility in search. While Anthropic has already adopted the signal, Google and Microsoft plan to support it next year. OpenAI says it will implement it in the “coming months.” Update, October 30th: Added a comment from Meta. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. MIT Technology Review
Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.
2026-10-30

Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinical psychologists, they can represent something more sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders. A new report suggests that the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up between 1990 and 2023—by a whopping 71%. By 2023, there were an estimated 18.1 million people living with one of these two disorders. What’s worse is that the number is likely to be much higher than that once we factor in other eating disorders. And, disturbingly, those in the field are already starting to see GLP-1 weight-loss drugs exacerbating the issue. Cheri Levinson is a clinical psychologist at the University of Louisville in Kentucky who has specialized in eating disorders for almost 20 years. She says she’s now seeing more patients with more severe symptoms than at any other time in her career, and that many of them are misusing GLP-1s. “This is the worst I’ve seen things,” she says. Eating disorders are mental health conditions in which people’s eating behaviors impact their health. They include anorexia nervosa (defined by a fear of weight gain), bulimia nervosa (in which people binge and purge), binge eating disorder, and others. It’s thought that some people have a genetic predisposition to certain eating disorders, but that some external factor is what triggers their onset. That might be stress, or social pressure to be skinny. Often, simply restricting food intake can trigger an eating disorder, says Levinson. Eating disorders—along with the pressure to look thin—were already prevalent in the 1990s (the heroin chic era springs to mind). To find out how things might have changed since then, Ruhai Bai at the Children’s Hospital of Nanjing Medical University in China and his colleagues pulled together data from studies and surveys conducted on populations around the world. The team used statistical models to fill in any data gaps. That research allowed the researchers to estimate the annual incidence of two eating disorders—anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa—for each of 204 countries and territories between 1990 and 2023. They also estimated the impact those disorders had on global health by calculating their disability-adjusted life years (one DALY represents a year of healthy life lost). The findings, published in JAMA Psychiatry on Wednesday, are unsettling. Between 1990 and 2023, there was a 71% increase in the incidence of those two eating disorders, the team found. Those conditions had a huge impact on global health, too. In 2023, they “cost the world the equivalent of about 3.83 million years of healthy life,” says Bai. Bai and his colleagues expect these figures to continue to rise. In 2023, an estimated 18.1 million people had either anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa; by 2050, the team predict, the two conditions could affect 21.8 million people. It gets worse. Those two conditions represent less than half of all eating disorders, says Levinson, who was not involved in Bai’s study. The true impact of these disorders is “probably three times the rate that they’re reporting,” she says. Bai and his colleagues didn’t explore the reasons for this rise (although Bai points out that it can’t be entirely explained by population growth). So how did we get here? Social media may have played a role. “Social media comparisons, influencers, challenges, diet trends can all be precipitating … events for an eating disorder,” says Cynthia Bulik, a clinical psychologist at the University of North Carolina, who was not involved in the new study. Bulik has been studying and treating eating disorders since the early 1980s. “Between 1990 and 2023 there has clearly been an upsurge of those types of environmental exposures,” she says. Many social media users are exposed to filtered and modified images of skinny influencers who may have undergone cosmetic surgery or extreme dieting and who are promoting “thinspiration” or “fitspiration” content. But users with eating disorders appear to be more likely to engage with this content—and to be fed more of it as a result. When researchers at the University of Melbourne investigated this phenomenon a couple of years ago, they found that the algorithms of TikTok users with eating disorders delivered 4343% more “toxic eating disorder videos” than those of users who didn’t have the disorders. They also got more content focused on dieting or exercising. Many were fed over 1000 problematic videos per month. Bulik describes social media as “quicksand” for people with, or at risk of, eating disorders. The covid-19 pandemic—and the social isolation that accompanied lockdowns—also appears to have contributed to the rise in eating disorders. Research conducted in 2021 found that the number of people hospitalized with eating disorders in the US doubled between 2018 and 2020. Researchers don’t yet have good data on how trends in eating disorders might have progressed since 2023. But anecdotally, things seem to be getting even worse. “There are more eating disorders, more severe eating disorders… and we’re seeing earlier ages of onset,” says Levinson. “We’re seeing eight- and nine-year-olds with eating disorders.” “From what I’m hearing and seeing, I am not going to be surprised if that number isn’t at least doubling again,” she adds. Levinson particularly worries about the impact of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which have exploded in popularity in recent years. A Gallup survey conducted earlier this year suggested that 15% of Americans had used one of these drugs for weight loss. And they’re becoming more accessible—drug companies are working on new formulations that are both easier to take and more effective, and many people can buy versions of these drugs from compound pharmacies or dubious online sources. In a small study published earlier this year, Levinson and her colleagues found that 30% of people with eating disorders had used GLP-1 drugs. The drugs might benefit some of those people. But 10% appeared to have misused the drugs. And Levinson thinks that figure is also an underestimate. Levinson says she and her colleagues have seen children develop eating disorders after being prescribed GLP-1s for medical reasons. They’ve seen others—even children as young as 11—buy versions of these drugs over TikTok. “We’re very concerned that GLPs will lead to even faster and more extreme weight loss in people with anorexia nervosa, and that patients will come to us in even more advanced stages of illness that become difficult to reverse,” says Bulik. Both Bulik and Levinson are continuing to study the impacts of these drugs in eating disorders. In the meantime, both urge medical professionals to consider the risks GLP-1s might pose to people with those disorders. Bulik contributed to a set of research and clinical guidelines, published earlier this month, that recommends doctors screen for eating disorders before prescribing the drugs, for example. The guidelines also recommend more patient education and more robust monitoring of people who take GLP-1s. If you think you or someone you know might have an eating disorder, you can find information and support at the National Eating Disorders Association. MIT Technology Review
We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time
2026-10-29

We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This summer, hundreds of OpenAI agents tried to cheat on a cybersecurity test. They broke out of their test environment and successfully hacked Hugging Face, a site that they believed held clues about how they’d be graded. Around the same time, another OpenAI agent accessed private files from an Australian government website. It’s a problem that’s by no means confined to OpenAI. In the months since, Anthropic, Google, and Meta have also disclosed incidents in which their agents have gone off track. And worryingly, in many cases the agents eluded the very researchers and safety teams tasked with monitoring them. AI agents are taking unauthorized actions, and the companies building and running them are mostly relying on luck and hindsight to mitigate any harmful impacts. They must do more to ensure safety. That’s why EU regulators and US agencies are rightfully calling for agent developers and deployers to monitor agents in real time. But these rules assume that companies can see what their agents are doing and why—when in reality, they lack sufficient visibility. As agents roll out across critical sectors, from finance to government to medicine, we must be able to monitor them in real time to prevent wide-ranging and potentially devastating consequences.  And while safety depends on monitoring, monitoring requires observability: the capacity to capture what the system did, process it into signals, and present those to humans or automated oversight systems. In other words, we need to know what the agent did and why it did it. And as our recent research shows, current agent frameworks—the software that runs agents— do not reliably generate the crucial observability records we need to ensure that agents are deployed safely. Agents present new kinds of problems that generative AI alone does not. They inherit the unpredictability of the LLMs they are built on, but where a generative AI system produces a single output for a human to act on, an agent performs longer tasks—it plans, picks tools, spawns sub-agents, and takes actions. So an agent’s course can take shape in a multitude of different ways, and no tests before launch can fully anticipate the risks. This makes observing the agents while they work even more challenging, and even more important. With colleagues from academia, civil society, and industry, we studied the four most widely used agent frameworks to see what signals they actually emit. We found major gaps that made it hard to achieve key safety goals, like noticing policy violations, unauthorized actions, and even human oversight failures. Key observability requirements were missing—like Agent IDs that persist over time, and records about which humans approved agent tasks. This made it hard to tell which agents did what or assess whether they were taking unauthorized actions. The good news is that these gaps, once identified, can be closed. Doing so starts with capturing two types of records: those that reveal what happened in an agent’s run and how the agent made its decisions. The “what happened” records identify the agent (while preserving privacy) and log how humans intervened and whether agent permissions or “memory” shifted, while the “decision” records show an agent’s reasoning and levels of confidence in the actions it’s taken. Take, for example, agents working to process a bank’s mortgage loans. The system examines an application and recommends denying it, and then a human loan officer signs off on the decision. In a later audit, or perhaps even in real time, a bank or regulator needs to be able to reconstruct which agent made the call, which officer approved it, and how long they took. And the bank must also analyze records of how the agent arrived at its refusal decision in order to ensure it did not use off-limits information, like details about the neighborhood the applicant is from. Recording “what happened” and “how the agent decided” require different approaches, but both are feasible. The current lack of “what happened” records is mostly a software plumbing problem that can be addressed through OpenTelemetry, an open observability standard maintained by engineers across the industry and by the agent frameworks adopting it. OpenTelemetry has launched a working group drafting shared formats for tracing agents, but they have yet to be finalized. The group should urgently incorporate standards for maintaining persistent agent identity and for recording human interventions and changes to agents’ permission or memory. And it should be done before these systems can scale, all the while attending to the privacy implications of agent ID that links to real people. Preserving “how agents decided” records is more challenging. The issue raises open research questions for model providers and researchers: Does the step-by-step reasoning a model displays, known as chain of thought, reflect how the model actually made a decision? And do we have reliable ways to measure how confident the agent is in that decision? These questions need answers before anyone sets standards for how such observability records are used. No single organization or sector can close the observability gaps on their own, but together, they can support the infrastructure required to prevent dangerous incidents and support safety. AI developers building agent frameworks, like OpenAI, Anthropic, CrewAI, and LangChain, must integrate telemetry signals recording an agent’s persistent identity, any human interventions, and any changes to an agent’s permissions and memory. Meanwhile, enterprises adopting these agent frameworks have an influential role to play; enterprises in regulated industries, like banks and hospitals, can put pressure on the agent developers to supply these missing observability records where standards do not yet exist. Enterprises must also define policies, permissions, and roles that set out what agents can safely do, since telemetry alone cannot assess whether agents broke specific rules. Finally, regulators and AI standards institutes must specify the observability records required for high-stakes AI deployments while leaving the technical details to industry. We have already seen initial momentum here: NIST’s agent standards initiative in the US is working on agent identity, one of the most salient gaps we found in our research. No bank would let a thousand unbadged employees loose on customer accounts and hope to piece together what happened when something goes wrong. Yet that’s how we’re deploying AI agents. Making their behavior observable is the first step toward fixing this. And by building the regulatory and policy ecosystem that makes it possible to act on what we see, we can hold organizations accountable. Until then, every task we hand to an agent hands over our agency, too. Madhulika Srikumar is the Head of the AI Safety Program at the Partnership on AI, a global non-profit partnership of leading industry, academic, and civil society organizations, advancing best practices in AI governance. Claire Leibowicz is the Director of AI, Trust, and Society at the Partnership on AI and a doctoral candidate at Oxford University. MIT Technology Review
Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot
2026-10-29

Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Climate change itself is increasingly fueling climate change. Rising concentrations of greenhouse gases are driving up global temperatures, setting off dangerous feedback loops that we have not fully accounted for in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming in various ways, but a growing number of observations and studies are at last providing a sharper sense of just how powerful these cascading effects could be. And, by extension, they’re showing how much faster the planet may warm in this century. A study in Environmental Research Letters in September, synthesizing the results of previous papers, found that the additional carbon dioxide and methane released from thawing permafrost, intensifying wildfires, hotter lakes and reservoirs, and wetter, warmer wetlands alone could drive global average temperatures up 0.2 °C to 0.4 °C this century. That would come on top of the 2.6°C of warming above preindustrial levels that modelers are already expecting through 2100. The additional heat could subject many of the world’s cities to monthslong heat waves and expose hundreds of millions of people to flooding. None of the models that inform the major UN climate reports, which assess how much the planet will likely warm under various emissions scenarios, account for all these feedback effects. Many of them don’t incorporate any. That leaves a huge blind spot in our understanding of how much the world could actually warm in the coming decades—and how rapidly nations will need to slash emissions to stay within critical boundaries. A separate analysis published in One Earth in March, which examined what the feedback problem means for our international climate goals, concluded that the world may be much further off track than is widely believed. It found that just three regional sources of warming-driven emissions—thawing permafrost, increasing fires in boreal forests, and warming tropical wetlands—may shorten by as much as 25% the time before the planet consistently breaches the 1.5 °C and 2 °C warming thresholds. Our climate models and the UN’s reports clearly need to be updated to better represent these effects. Nations must also step up their efforts to reduce emissions to reflect this improving understanding of the dangers we face, says Sam Abernethy, the lead author of the paper and a climate scientist at Spark Climate Solutions, a San Francisco–based nonprofit research group. At least some of this is starting to happen. Spark kick-started an initiative, called the Warming-Induced Greenhouse Gas Emissions Model Intercomparison Project, through which more than 20 international teams are running the same set of analyses on different models to develop greater understanding of feedback effects’ potential power. Researchers involved hope that the UN will incorporate those results into its next major climate assessment, due out in 2029. That report will update estimates of the remaining emissions that can be released before the world crosses key temperature thresholds. But what is already clear is that the planet is rapidly warming and that nations haven’t done nearly enough to cut fossil-fuel pollution. And even if we did manage to halt it, warming-driven emissions could continue. So a number of the research groups involved in the recent studies have started to explore what nations could do to get feedback emissions under control. Scientists at the Woodwell Climate Research Center in Massachusetts are, for example, investigating the possibility of preventing fires or rapidly detecting them and putting them out when they threaten to burn the vegetation and soil that help insulate particularly carbon-rich wedges of permafrost (a subject I explored at length in a recent feature story). In addition, the Environmental Defense Fund’s Climate Innovation Initiative has backed lab research on the potential of adding sulfate, iron, or other compounds to wetlands to counteract fast-rising methane emissions. Several startups are also exploring possibilities. They’re looking into suppressing the lightning strikes that are igniting more and more fires in high latitudes, placing synthetic mats above permafrost to prevent it from melting, and deploying autonomous drones to quickly suppress potentially high-emissions fires before they burn out of control. Investigating all these ideas is worthwhile. But researchers are only in the earliest stages of figuring out how big a dent in emissions any such efforts could achieve across sprawling global ecosystems undergoing massive transformations. The surest path to reducing the effect of warming-driven warming remains the one we’ve known all along: breaking society’s addiction to fossil fuels. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
Creating AI value with enterprise context
2026-10-28

Creating AI value with enterprise context

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Across a business, any number of AI platforms, systems, and tools may be at work simultaneously—for example, AI-powered customer platforms, conversational assistants, and workflow and process automation tools. Each one is sophisticated, advanced, and quick to respond. But, when set to work, the outcomes and actions that emerge may be wrong, confused, or lacking a proper understanding of the business. Many organizations find themselves in this situation—and it could be one of key reasons they struggle to extract returns on their AI investments. An MIT Technology Review Insights poll finds that while every organization surveyed is using AI today to some degree, fewer than one in five (16%) are extracting transformative value from their deployments. The largest percentage of respondents (40%) say they are seeing moderate gains in specific tasks or teams, while 21% report meaningful gains, leaving 23% that are seeing minimal returns or none at all. Companies are mostly still piloting AI use cases (according to 38% of respondents) or using it to assist employees’ work with tools like co-pilots or chat assistants (35%). Just 12% of enterprises have AI agents in production and 3% say they are using AI to do most of the work in key processes. “There’s a big gap between what the models are capable of and how the models are actually being useful inside organizations,” says Prukalpa Sankar, co-founder of Atlan. “For every real-life intent and purpose, artificial general intelligence is already here. It’s just not really useful in most companies today.” The missing layer, Sankar says, is context, or how well AI understands a business. A context layer is the architectural infrastructure that sits between an organization’s business systems (for example, systems of record and data, and its AI platforms). It translates enterprise intelligence into governed, machine-usable context for AI agents or models and helps knowledge compound. “Without a context layer for AI, it’s almost like you’re hiring the smartest intern in the world, blindfolding them, and throwing them into your organization,” Sankar says. This content was produced by Insights, MIT Technology Review’s custom content arm, not its editorial staff. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. An exclusive interview with Nick Cammarata, the researcher whose 26,000-word viral blog post has AI safety folks celebrating. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. Surveillance towers, increasingly powered by AI, promised faster responses to crossings. Our investigation of deaths near the towers found they are failing to deliver. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. Google, Microsoft, OpenAI and Anthropic back one AI training opt-out
2026-10-31

Google, Microsoft, OpenAI and Anthropic back one AI training opt-out

The Next Web | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Google, Microsoft, OpenAI and Anthropic will honour one shared AI training opt-out in robots.txt. It lets a website keep its content out of generative AI training with a single rule. Today, a site that wants to say no has to write separate rules for each company’s crawler. The shared signal comes from an IETF draft by Kevin Kelley of Anthropic, published on Friday. The same day, Google, OpenAI and Microsoft announced they would support it. Kelley announced the draft on the IETF’s AI preferences mailing list. It works through robots.txt, the file sites already use to tell crawlers what they may fetch. A site adds a group for a new “policy token”, __usage_ai-training. It then lists the paths it does not want used for training. The draft’s example blocks a whole site except its press kit. “Publishers should not have to choose between being discoverable in Bing and deciding how generative models use their work,” Krishna Madhavan, principal product manager for Microsoft Bing, wrote. Anthropic has already started work on it, Kelley wrote. He said it would complement Anthropic’s existing crawler controls rather than replace them. “It’ll take us some time to add support across our services and work through the inevitable complications and edge cases that I’m sure we’ll discover,” Kelley wrote. OpenAI said it aims to implement the signal “in the coming months”. Its existing crawler controls, such as GPTBot, will still apply, it said. Google said it aims to support the signal in 2027, and its crawler documentation says early 2027. Google-Extended is Google’s own AI training control. If a site opts out with the token and does not mention Google-Extended, Google will treat it as a block on Google-Extended. Sites that allow Google-Extended can appear in Gemini app answers and get clicks, Google Search Advocate John Mueller wrote. Microsoft will honour the new preferences from 1 February 2027. They will replace the NoArchive and NoCache tags it has used since 2023. Microsoft is also adding a separate page-level setting for grounding, when an AI answer draws on a live web page. One option allows Microsoft’s own products, such as Copilot. It blocks the grounding services Microsoft sells to other companies. The draft says the token states a preference about how sites want their content used. It is not an order about crawling. A site with no such group has expressed no preference, and existing rules for all crawlers do not count. Private agreements between a site and an AI company can override it. The draft is an individual submission. The IETF’s datatracker says the IETF has not endorsed it and it has no formal standing in the standards process. Microsoft said the new preferences “do not establish licensing or payment arrangements”. Blocking AI grounding “will significantly reduce your content’s visibility in AI-generated experiences”, it added. It does not change a page’s place in Bing search results. Meta communications head Andy Stone voiced support for a common signal on X, The Verge reported. He did not say whether Meta would honour it. Google said the proposal is consistent with the EU’s Code of Practice for general-purpose AI under the AI Act. The code recognises robots.txt as a main way for publishers to state machine-readable preferences. Among the people the draft thanks for shaping the discussion are staff from the Financial Times, the BBC, Bertelsmann and the European Publishers Council. Publishers have also pushed for payment. In July, Cloudflare told AI crawlers to pay or get blocked. Its chief executive has warned that bots could reach 1,000 times human web traffic. In Australia, the ABC and SBS want AI companies to pay for news. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Sam Altman meets Samsung’s presidents days after his DRAM remark

The Next Web | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Sam Altman speaks with Samsung presidents at Samsung’s training centre in Yongin, South Korea, 31 October 2026 Samsung hosted OpenAI chief executive Sam Altman for talks with about 50 of its presidents on Saturday. Two days earlier, a podcast went out in which Sam Altman said it is “quite terrible” for the world’s limited DRAM to sit in people’s own computers. DRAM is the memory chip Samsung makes. Samsung set out the details in a statement on its Korean newsroom. The presidents came from its electronics, finance and biotech affiliates, and met Altman at Samsung’s training centre in Yongin, south of Seoul. The talk ran under the theme of how AI will change the next ten years, and what companies should choose to do. Samsung’s presidents and Altman looked at where AI technology is heading and how it can be used, Samsung said. They also traded views on the new markets and business opportunities it could create. They discussed how AI will change decision-making and the way organisations run, and what skills managers will need, the statement said. Samsung did not publish anything Altman said, and it did not name the Samsung executives in the room. Samsung said the session was part of its training for presidents. Its view is that a company’s strength in the AI era comes from its managers’ judgement and execution, so the presidents are meant to lead by learning AI themselves. Before meeting Altman, the presidents took a hands-on generative AI course, their second after one in June. They used AI to sort scattered management documents into their own “knowledge repository”, and practised using AI in decisions, Samsung said. Bloomberg’s Yoolim Lee reported that Altman is the latest American tech leader to visit South Korea. Nvidia’s Jensen Huang and AMD’s Lisa Su have each visited twice since last year to strengthen supply ties, Bloomberg reported. Samsung Electronics and OpenAI signed a letter of intent on AI infrastructure in October 2025, Samsung said. Since then, Samsung has worked to supply memory chips for OpenAI’s Stargate data centre project. Under the same agreement, Samsung SDS is to work with OpenAI on AI data centres and AI services for businesses. Samsung C&T and Samsung Heavy Industries are developing floating data centres together. Samsung Electronics chairman Lee Jae-yong visited OpenAI’s San Francisco headquarters in July, the statement added. In June, OpenAI said Samsung Electronics would roll out ChatGPT Enterprise and Codex to its employees in South Korea and in its global Device eXperience division. Samsung has been bringing AI into research, production, marketing and support work since June, it said. Each affiliate has its own AI team. About 2,300 executives have been trained so far, and all employees are due to finish AI training by February 2027. Altman made the remark to Stripe co-founder John Collison on Collison’s podcast, Cheeky Pint, released on Thursday. TNW covered the episode when it came out, leading on his plan to point OpenAI’s robots at building data centres. Collison asked why high prices for memory and chips had not yet brought more supply. Altman said memory makers were now “each going to make like $100 billion of free cash flow in a year”. Their history of near-bankruptcy from building too many factories keeps them careful with spending, he said. He then turned to people who want to run AI models on their own machines. “A lot of people want local inference. And somehow, they assume that the problem is solved now,” Altman said. Those people expect open-source models to run on their own computers. They think data centres will then need less, he said. “From a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time,” Altman said. “You want maximum utilization in the cloud if you’re trying to deal with this very supply constrained environment,” he said. Many people’s intuition is the opposite of that, he added. SK Hynix said this week that memory shortages will last to 2030. Samsung posted a record quarterly profit on AI memory demand on Thursday. Apple chief executive John Ternus has called the shortage a crisis, TIME reported. Later in the episode, Altman said the cost of chips and energy should fall. For that to happen, he said, supply has “got to go way up”. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Anthropic denies breach as tampered Claude Code extension hits Cursor users
2026-10-31

Anthropic denies breach as tampered Claude Code extension hits Cursor users

The Next Web | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
A tampered copy of Anthropic’s Claude Code extension sat in Cursor’s extension marketplace for about 11 hours on Thursday 29 October. It reached up to 5,000 users. The malicious versions tried to plant an SSH backdoor on developers’ machines, Cursor said in a security notice. Anthropic says nobody breached its own systems. A security firm that studied the attack says the extension went out through Anthropic’s official account on Cursor. Neither Anthropic nor Cursor has said how the attacker got access. The affected versions were 2.1.312 and 2.1.313 for Windows and macOS. They were live from about 2:27am to 1:35pm Pacific time. Cursor, which SpaceX acquired earlier this year, said Anthropic did not develop the code. The extension collected details about each machine and its user, including the hostname, username, Git email and public IP address. It then tried to add the attacker’s SSH key and turn on the computer’s SSH service. It also tried to open a reverse tunnel to the attacker’s server. That last step needs administrator rights, which the extension lacks on most macOS and Windows machines. So it would usually fail, Cursor said. But the key, the persistence scripts and the data upload still went through. Uninstalling the extension may not be enough to remove them. We identified a security incident affecting a modified Claude Code VS Code extension accessed through our Cursor IDE extension marketplace, impacting at most 5,000 users. This malicious version of the extension was not developed by Anthropic. It does not impact other extensions downloaded from the http://cursor.com/marketplace, Grok Bot, Grok or our other SpaceXSI products. In response, we took down our extension marketplace this afternoon, worked with relevant providers to neutralize the malware, and have reinstated the marketplace after confirming there is no further risk. If affected, please review instructions in this blog post to understand how to find the malicious package and establish a path to mitigate any potential impact. We are asking all customers update to Claude Code for VS Code version 2.1.314: https://cursor.com/blog/claude-code-extension-security-notice Cursor removed the versions and shut its whole marketplace while it checked the other extensions. It then rotated its keys and reopened it. It also had the attacker’s server taken offline. It has published scripts to check for and remove the backdoor. Felix Rieseberg, a member of Anthropic’s technical staff, responded in a post on X. We’re working with Cursor on this incident, but it wasn’t a compromise of Anthropic’s systems or employee laptops. A malicious actor used access to Cursor’s marketplace to publish a tampered copy of the Claude Code extension, which we didn’t build or publish. The versions on our official channels are not affected and this only impacts Cursor users. Felix Rieseberg (@felixrieseberg) October 30, 2026 The security firm Phorion first flagged the compromise. Its co-founder and chief technology officer, Luke Roberts, told Ars Technica that the attacker reached the official distribution channel. “The malicious version 2.1.313 was posted to the official Anthropic account, effectively superseding 2.1.312,” Roberts told Ars. Independent researcher Kevin Beaumont made the same point, Dan Goodin reported for Ars. Extensions update automatically in Cursor by default, Roberts said. Cursor has told users to move to a clean version, 2.1.314. The malware also sent the names of up to 40 environment variables matching keys, tokens and secrets, but not their values, Phorion found. That gives the attacker a quick list of which cloud credentials sit on each machine. Supply-chain attacks on developer tools have hit npm packages this year, and researchers have warned that AI coding tools could amplify such attacks. A Claude agent ran inside a Pentagon network for two months, WIRED reports
2026-10-31

A Claude agent ran inside a Pentagon network for two months, WIRED reports

The Next Web | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
An Accenture contractor connected a sensitive Pentagon system to their personal Claude Code account. The AI agent then ran on a Defense Department network for two months, Vittoria Elliott reported for WIRED on Thursday 29 October, citing sources. The agent appears to have sent login credentials and a network map to a high-risk website, the report says. Accenture denied wrongdoing. TNW has not independently verified the report. “These allegations were thoroughly and independently investigated and found to be without merit. We fully complied with our legal and contractual obligations,” an Accenture spokesperson told WIRED. Accenture did not say who carried out the investigation, WIRED reported. The Defense Department told WIRED that it does not comment on the status of its networks or systems. The system holds controlled unclassified information (CUI), which is sensitive data that needs review before any release. Several intelligence agencies use it. It runs on NIPRNet, the Pentagon’s network for unclassified information. NIPRNet connects to the internet but has heavy security. The contractor gave the Claude agent their login credentials so it could write code and set up cloud servers, a source told WIRED. That gave the agent administrative access and let it get around the normal login controls. The agent wrote code and saved it into the system for the contractor. It also had to reach the internet to do its tasks. In doing so, it appears to have sent the credentials, passwords and a network map to a third-party website. WIRED did not name the site. The US National Institute of Standards and Technology has flagged that site as high risk, WIRED reported. A flaw there could let hackers steer an AI coding agent into stealing credentials. “It’s 100 percent a national security threat and could cause irreparable harm,” a source aware of the incident told WIRED. Defense Department staff and federal contractors must report cybersecurity incidents within 72 hours. Accenture did not first report this one to the government at all, WIRED’s sources said. Its staff did not treat it as urgent. Pentagon staff currently may not use Claude, after the department labelled Anthropic a supply chain risk. Anthropic pointed WIRED to its usage policy, which bars using its products to get around security controls without authorisation. There is no sign that hackers reached a classified system, WIRED reported. But teams often test code on NIPRNet before it moves to classified networks, said Jake Williams. He is a former NSA hacker who teaches at IANS. “The code could operate completely normally in NIPR and do something malicious and undisclosed in a SIPR network, that is 100 percent possible,” Williams told WIRED. WIRED’s editors discussed the scoop on its Uncanny Valley podcast on Friday 30 October. Leah Feiger, WIRED’s director of politics and science, said the incident was new. “We have not seen this before,” Feiger said. Contributing editor Zoë Schiffer noted that there is no sign of a hacking attempt. The contractor was trying to use Claude to write and deploy code, she said. “We don’t know that anything nefarious was going on,” Schiffer said. Executive editor Brian Barrett called it “inexcusable”, and said he assumed it would not be the last case of its kind. Accenture holds more than $3.5bn in federal contracts, nearly $605m of them with the Pentagon, according to WIRED. Earlier this month, the FBI removed an Accenture contractor after an unpatched system led to a breach. Accenture also caps staff Claude spending at $29 a month, 404 Media reported in October. John Ternus tells WIRED Apple won’t ship AI for AI’s sake

The Next Web | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Apple chief executive John Ternus poses for a selfie with customers in an Apple Store. Apple wants people to love its products without having to think about whether AI made them work, chief executive John Ternus told Steven Levy for WIRED. The interview was published on Friday 30 October. Levy spoke to Ternus at Apple’s Los Angeles launch of the touchscreen MacBook Pro this week. Ternus, 51, has spent 25 years at Apple and has been chief executive for less than two months. “We don’t want to ship AI for AI’s sake,” Ternus told WIRED. He said he hoped people would see that Apple’s work is building products that make their lives better. Apple is “absolutely going to” use AI to do that, he said. “When we’re doing it right, people won’t have to think about whether it was AI that made this happen or more traditional software. What matters is, did we build something that they really love?” he said. Levy asked about AI agents that could replace today’s ways of using a computer. More work would then happen on its own. “That could make life really easy, I suppose, but the creation of products is a deeply human and personal thing, something that we love to do,” Ternus said. He did not say whether he favours AI regulation. He said he reads the stories and thinks about them “from a cybersecurity standpoint”. “Clearly these models are very capable of doing things,” he said. “Our focus is on making sure that our products are secure and safe, and protecting the users’ information,” he said. Tim Cook, who handed over to Ternus on 1 September, is now Apple’s executive chairman. Ternus said he consults him. “Tim is available to help wherever I ask him, and it’s great to have him in that role,” Ternus said. Asked which business leaders inspire him, Ternus said he had not really thought about it. He said he had only “a handful of interactions” with Steve Jobs, but has always felt like “sort of a student of Steve’s teachings”. He said one privilege of the job is learning many new things, and that he will keep going deeper into new areas. “But, you know, I do love product. I’m super passionate about it,” he said. Two of his executives said the same. Johny Srouji, Apple’s chief hardware officer, called Ternus “a product guy at heart”. Greg Joswiak, head of worldwide marketing, said Ternus has a great sense for great products. “You can’t teach that,” Joswiak told WIRED. Ternus earlier told TIME that the memory chip shortage is a crisis. Bloomberg reported in September that he is cutting managers to ship products faster.
